 ABSTRACT
This document presents the facility-recommended characterization of the neutron, prompt gamma
ray, and delayed gamma ray radiation fields at the Godiva IV critical assembly at the National Criticality
Experiments Research Center (NCERC). The environments assessed include the In-Core location, a
location on the Top Hat, 1m away from the assembly, and 2m away from the assembly. The neutron,
prompt gamma ray, and delayed gamma ray energy spectra, uncertainties, and covariance matrices are
presented as well as radial and axial neutron and gamma ray fluence profiles on the Top Hat
surrounding the critical assembly. Recommended constants are given to facilitate the conversion of
various dosimetry readings into radiation metrics desired by experimenters. Representative pulse
operations are presented with conversion examples.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
Godiva IV is a fast burst critical assembly constructed of approximately 65 kg of highly enriched
uranium (HEU) alloyed with 1.5 percent molybdenum for strength. The cylindrical core is nominally
six inches tall and seven inches in diameter. Godiva IV was designed and built in 1967, following
several earlier incarnations of uranium burst assemblies. Godiva IV is one of the last such critical
assemblies in the United States and can be used for studies of super-prompt critical behavior as well
as irradiations and demonstrations. The Godiva IV reactor cell has large areas of testing space
available with test locations that include both in-core and ex-core. In Figure 1, the four characterized
test locations are shown as white spheres relative to the Godiva IV assembly.

 Figure 1. MCNP rendered schematic of the Godiva IV device with characterized positions.

To provide a characterization of neutron fluence and gamma dose rates at the test locations, the
neutron, prompt gamma ray, and delayed gamma ray energy spectra and fluence per fission were
calculated using a surface tally of a volumetric space for the central cavity or a 6-cm diameter tally
sphere for the ex-core locations. The results of the MCNP calculation of the Godiva IV in-core and
ex-core free-field locations form an initial representation of the neutron spectrum. For this work, the
neutron energy spectrum was calculated with both a 640-group and an 89-group energy structure.

An improved spectrum can then be obtained by combining this initial trial spectrum with measured
integral values that correspond to the reaction rates from high-fidelity passive dosimetry reactions. A
total of 195 activation foils were irradiated across four test locations: In-Core, Top Hat, 1m, and 2m
from the assembly, utilizing 46 different reactions. The Sandia LSL-M2, heavily modified version of
the LSL code, and IRDFF-II dosimetry cross sections were employed in the unfolding analysis to
derive neutron spectra with their associated uncertainties starting with the a priori 640-energy group
trial spectra. The resulting least-squares adjusted neutron fluence spectrum includes an energy-
dependent uncertainty. The spectrum can then be considered “characterized,” in that it quantifies
the “true” neutron-fluence energy spectrum with a stated accuracy, including energy dependent
uncertainties and a covariance matrix.

 4

Godiva IV bursts are commonly referred to by the temperature rise, ΔT, measured near the center
of the core. Three typical burst sizes are performed on Godiva IV, corresponding to 70 °C, 150 °C
and 250 °C. Table 1 provides a short summary of the dose rates and fluence at the four locations
for a nominal 250 °C burst. 1 MJ corresponds to 127 °C temperature rise.

 Table 1. Metrics for Godiva IV 250°C ΔT Pulse

 Metric In-Core Top Hat 1m 2m
Total Neutron Fluence ([n/cm2])                          5.67E+14    1.86E+13   1.16E+12   6.52E+11
Total Neutron Silicon Dose (rad[Si])                     3.75E+04    1.26E+03   6.38E+01   2.38E+01
Prompt Neutron Ionizing Silicon Dose (rad[Si])           2.19E+04    7.42E+02   3.74E+01   1.37E+01
Prompt Gamma-Ray Ionizing Silicon Dose (rad[Si])         2.18E+05    2.45E+03   1.46E+02   8.27E+01
Delayed Gamma-Ray Ionizing Silicon Dose (rad[Si])        7.29E+04    1.46E+03   6.62E+01   2.26E+01
Total Ionizing Si Dose (rad[Si])                         3.13E+05    4.65E+03   249.40     119.00

Metrics normalized to 1 MJ of reactor energy yield are summarized below for the four locations and
provided throughout the paper. These values can be converted to burst size using 1 MJ equal to
nominally 100 °C temperature rise.

 • Key Fluence Metrics:
 § In-Core Location: The neutron fluence at the axial fluence maximum is approximately
 2.8 × 1014 n/cm2 per MJ of reactor energy yield, with 0.0007% below 1 keV and 95%
 above 100 keV. The prompt gamma-ray dose is approximately 5.7 × 104 rad(Si) per MJ,
 and the delayed gamma-ray dose is 3.7 × 104 rad(Si) per MJ.

 § Top Hat Location: The neutron fluence is approximately 7.3 × 1012 n/cm2 per MJ,
 with 1.3% below 1 keV and 94% above 100 keV. The prompt gamma-ray dose is
 approximately 9.6 × 102 rad(Si) per MJ, and the delayed gamma-ray dose is 5.7 × 102
 rad(Si) per MJ

 § 1m and 2m Locations: The neutron fluence is approximately 6.3 × 1011 n/cm2 per MJ
 of reactor energy and 2.5 × 1011 n/cm2, respectively. The prompt gamma-ray doses are
 approximately 78 rad(Si) per MJ at 1m and 33 rad(Si) per MJ at 2m, with delayed gamma-
 ray doses of 35 rad(Si) per MJ at 1m and 9 rad(Si) per MJ at 2m.

1. INTRODUCTION
Characterization of the neutron and gamma-ray environments in and around the Godiva IV critical
assembly is important to support high fidelity experiments for nuclear criticality, nuclear data
validation, dosimetry testing, and radiation shielding benchmarks. Characterization of Godiva IV has
included both modeling and experimental efforts. Redundant accurate neutronic models of Godiva
IV can be used by experimenters, for planning and designing experiments, and in assessing
experimental results. Experimental observations, including passive and active dosimetry, are important
to determine the accuracy of the model, the energy dependent neutron fluence, the conversion
constants for radiation metrics, the axial and radial neutron and gamma-ray fluence profiles, and the
time-dependent responses for different pulse sizes are presented.

For this work a total of 195 foils were irradiated resulting in 46 different reactions, were irradiated at
all 4 test locations: In-Core, Top Hat, 1m, and 2m. Sandia’s version of the LSL-M2 code and the
IRDFF-II dosimetry cross sections, were used in the unfolding analysis [1].

This document presents the laboratory-recommended characterizations of the radiation fields at the
Godiva IV critical assembly. These radiation fields include the In-Core location; three leakage free-
field locations with a location on the Top Hat, 1m away from the assembly, and 2m away from the
assembly, all at the axial fluence maximum at the median of the core height. The free-field
environment is the radiation environment external to the Godiva IV Top Hat, within the reactor cell
without the presence of any spectrum modifying materials.

The neutron, prompt gamma-ray, and delayed gamma-ray energy spectra, and uncertainties are
presented at the In-Core location, a location on the Top Hat, 1m away from the assembly, and 2m
away from the assembly, at roughly 3.25 inches above the bottom of the Top Hat, at the identified
peak neutron fluence location available. In addition, radial and axial neutron, and gamma-ray fluence
profiles are provided within the available experiment area around Godiva IV at the 1m and 2m
locations around the assembly. Recommended constants are given that facilitate the conversion of
various dosimetry readings into radiation metrics desired by experimenters.

The neutronics model for Godiva IV was modeled in MCNPÒ1 by LANL. Models of Godiva IV were
created by Russ Mostellar when it was located at TA-18 and recently updated by Jeff Favorite. In 2023,
Curtis D. Peters of SNL made minor additions to the Jeff Favorite model to perform the required
calculations for these characterizations. Details of the neutronic model and calculations are described
later in Appendix A. A 640-group and 89-group neutron energy spectra and a 48-group gamma-ray
energy spectrum was calculated using MCNP6.3. MCNP calculations used the ENDF/B-VIII.0 cross
sections for the Godiva IV model with scoring spheres or boundary placed at the In-Core location,
the Top Hat, 1m away from the assembly, and 2m away from the assembly. The calculated neutron
spectra can then be used with activation foil measurements and dosimetry cross sections, as the trial
function in the SNL unfolding codes GenSpec or LSL-M2. Sandia’s LSL-M2 is a heavily modified
version of LSL [14]. Both SNL unfolding codes used herein are Sandia Proprietary or are not approved
for public release, and therefore not available for general download.

1 MCNP® and Monte Carlo N-Particle® are registered trademarks owned by Triad National Security, LLC, manager and operator of
Los Alamos National Laboratory. Any third party use of such registered marks should be properly attributed to Triad National
Security, LLC, including the use of the designation as appropriate. For the purposes of visual clarity, the registered trademark symbol
is assumed for all references to MCNP within the remainder of this paper.

 14

2. ENVIRONMENT DESCRIBTION

2.1. Godiva IV Description
Godiva IV is a fast burst critical assembly constructed of approximately 65 kg of highly enriched
uranium (HEU) fuel alloyed with 1.5 percent molybdenum for strength [16]. The cylindrical core is
nominally six inches tall and seven inches in diameter. Godiva IV was designed and built in 1967,
following several earlier incarnations of uranium burst assemblies. Godiva IV is one of the last such
critical assemblies in the United States and can be used for studies of super-prompt critical behavior
as well as irradiations and demonstrations.

The cylindrical core of the Godiva IV assembly contains six stacked, interlocking rings, a two-part
inner sub-assembly plate, a safety block, two control rods, and a burst rod. These components are
composed of alloyed highly enriched uranium (HEU) and molybdenum (1.5%). The current fuel was
aluminum-ion plated in 1979 or 1980 in the same manner as the previous fuel had been in 1976
[17][18]. The coating minimizes oxidation of the fuel, reducing both contamination and loss of fuel
over time. A photo of the core is shown as Figure 2.

Figure 2. Godiva IV without Top Hat radiological cover, seen are the fuel plates, the c-clamps and
 the Saturn ring.

From 1967-2004, the Los Alamos Critical Experiment Facility (LACEF) team conducted almost 2000
bursts on Godiva IV at Los Alamos National Laboratory's Technical Area 18 (TA-18). In 2004,
Godiva operations ceased and LACEF began the arduous process of relocating the critical assemblies
to the Device Assembly Facility (DAF) at the Nevada National Security Site (NNSS). In 2005, the
Godiva IV core was disassembled, then reassembled for reference, and then finally disassembled and
packaged for shipment to the NNSS. This reassembly was performed to document the entire process,
to ensure the core was assembled correctly after the transfer, and to take physical measurements of
the core.

 15

Following refurbishment of the stand and control system, the components of Godiva IV were shipped
to Nevada to be operated at the newly created National Criticality Experiments Research Center
(NCERC) within the Device Assembly Facility (DAF) at the Nevada National Security Site (NNSS).
Godiva IV was reassembled there in 2012 and burst operations resumed in 2013. Since then, Godiva
IV has performed more than 100 bursts as well as delayed critical and subcritical operations for a
variety of experiments.

As for the general layout of Godiva IV, a steel spindle holds the inner subassembly plates together.
The hollow region in the spindle has historically been called the glory hole but will be referred to as
the central cavity in this report. Experimental materials, such as foils, can be placed in the central
cavity for high flux irradiation.

The spindle, the alignment pins, the shim ring, and the base for the safety block are made from steel.
C-clamps composed of a high-strength maraging steel hold the fuel together [16]. The clamps are
attached to support legs bolted to the stand. A 'Saturn Ring' surrounds the clamps and exerts inward
pressure to keep the core together. A cross section of the core is shown in Figure 3. Also shown are
the two resistance temperature detectors (RTDs) used to monitor the temperature of the system
during an operation.

A CAD model of the entire Godiva IV assembly is shown in Figure 4. The Top Hat, an optional,
aluminum, contamination-minimizing shroud over the fuel is shown transparently so the fuel is still
visible. It has a nominal outer diameter of 20 inches, a nominal height of 12 inches, and a wall thickness
of 0.08 inches.

 Figure 3. Cross-section image of Godiva-IV.

 16

 Figure 4. CAD Model rendering of Godiva IV, displaying the assembled core and stand.

A fast neutron fluence spectrum is found at the Godiva IV In-Core location and at all the measured
ex-core locations.

Godiva IV can be operated above delayed critical at steady-state for varying power levels or in a free
run configuration where a positive period is configured and the assembly increases in power until the
temperature feedback mechanisms decrease reactivity to the point where power decreases. It can also
be operated by creating a super-prompt critical state with the rapid insertion of a rod to create a sharp
burst of radiation. This too is terminated by temperature feedback although on a much shorter
timescale, causing a shock wave to emanate from the center of the core and disengage the
electromagnet that holds the safety block in place.

Godiva IV bursts are commonly referred to by the temperature rise, ΔT, measured near the center of
the core. Three typical burst sizes are performed on Godiva IV, corresponding to 70 °C, 150 °C and
250 °C. All have initial periods and FWHM in the 10’s to 100’s of microsecond range. Typical yields
are in the 1016 fissions per burst. A similar number of fissions can be generated at a steady-state power
level over the time frame of hours. For this work, both bursts and steady-state operations were used

 17

to irradiate the fission and activation foils depending on fission or activation product lifetimes and
operational considerations.

Godiva IV maintains a fast neutron fluence spectrum In-Core and in all the leakage ex-core locations.
For an unmoderated condition, the neutron fluence in the central cavity position, at the axial fluence
maximum is ~2.8E+14 n/cm2 per MJ of reactor energy yield. The 1-MeV Damage-Equivalent Silicon
(DES) fluence is ~2.5E+14 n/cm2 per MJ of reactor energy. The prompt gamma-ray dose at the same
position is ~5.7E+04 rad(Si) per MJ. The delayed gamma-ray dose is 3.7E+04 rad(Si) per MJ.

For an unmoderated condition, the neutron fluence at the Top Hat location, at the axial fluence
maximum is ~7.3E+12 n/cm2 per MJ of reactor energy. The 1-MeV Damage-Equivalent Silicon
(DES) fluence is ~6.5E+12 n/cm2 per MJ of reactor energy. The prompt gamma-ray dose at the same
position is ~9.6E+02 rad(Si) per MJ. The delayed gamma-ray dose is ~5.7E+02 rad(Si) per MJ.

Lastly, for an unmoderated condition, the neutron fluence at the 1m and 2m locations external to the
assembly, at the axial fluence maximum is ~6.3E+11 n/cm2 per MJ of reactor energy and ~2.5E+11
n/cm2 per MJ of reactor energy, respectively. The 1-MeV Damage-Equivalent Silicon (DES) fluence
is ~4.5E+11 n/cm2 per MJ of reactor energy and ~1.3E+11 n/cm2 per MJ of reactor energy,
respectively. The prompt gamma-ray dose at the 1m position is ~78 rad(Si) per MJ and ~33 rad(Si)
per MJ at the 2m position. The delayed gamma-ray dose is ~35 rad(Si) per MJ at 1m and ~9 rad(Si)
per MJ at 2m.

The preceding metrics are provided for reference when experimenters are combining metrics found
subsequently in this report.

2.1.1. In-Core, Top Hat, 1m, and 2m Free-Field Locations
Godiva IV was designed as critical assembly, intended to study the effects of materials interactions
within nuclear systems at the National Criticality Experiments Research Center (NCERC). Godiva IV
is currently located at the National Criticality Experiments Research Center (NCERC) part of the
Device Assembly Facility (DAF) at the Nevada National Security Site (NNSS). The Godiva IV
building can be neutronically approximated using the following information. The room is cylindrical
with an inner radius of ~17 ft. (5.1816 m) and a wall thickness of ~1 ft. (30.48 cm). There is an opening
to the room that has a width of 8 ft. (2.4384 m). The entire facility is covered by compacted earth,
with a thick gravel overlaying the roof. Figure 5 shows the dimensions along with the second critical
assembly machine located in the building, Comet. The experiment room opening is considered
“North”, Comet is on the “East” end of the room, Godiva IV is on the “West” end of the room, and
the round portion at the bottom of the figure is considered the “South” side. These directions are
labeled in Figure 6.

 18

Figure 5. Schematic of the reactor cell showing both Godiva IV (left) and Comet (right).

 19

The Godiva IV reactor cell has large areas of testing space available with test locations that include
both in-core and ex-core. Within Godiva IV is the In-Core testing locating, called the central cavity.
The central cavity allows for irradiations to occur at both high neutron fluence and high total ionizing
dose. Three ex-core positions have been evaluated at Godiva IV to provide adequate testing spaces
with neutron metrics. In Figure 6, the characterized test locations are show in reference to the Godiva
IV assembly.

 Figure 6. MCNP rendered schematic of the Godiva-IV device with characterized positions. The 6
 cm tally sphere locations can be noted, with the 2 m location in the northeast corner of the room
 and the 1 m location in the southern corner of the room.

For all of the ex-core testing locations, the axial fluence maximum from the Godiva IV core was
determined using a sulfur mapping of key areas surrounding the core performed in 2022. The sulfur
mapping ensures the selected testing location has a uniform neutron fluence. Figure 7 shows an image
of the sulfur map, and the area determined to have the best uniformity on the Top Hap surface. The
metrics presented in this report, for the unmoderated condition at each location, remain valid such as
the 1-MeV damage-equivalent silicon fluence and the delayed gamma ray dose for experiments
conducted at these specified four characterized positions. Outside of the test areas where the fluence
varies by only ± 5%, the fluence varies across a 1-ft by 1-ft testing area of no more than 9% variation
in the fluence.

 20

 Figure 7. Sulfur map used to identify test location on the Top Hat.

2.1.2. Godiva IV MCNP Model
The MCNP model of Godiva IV is shown in Figure 8. The input model is included in Appendix A of
this document. The MCNP model of Godiva IV includes all of the fuel rings as well as the control
rods, burst rod, and safety block that make up the core. The control rods and safety block can be
adjusted in the vertical direction in the model. Typically, calculations are performed with the safety
block and control rods adjusted to the approximate delayed critical (DC) position. The calculated DC
position can be found for the model by iterating on the control rod position until keff is equal to one.
The model is typically run using ENDF.VII cross-section data at room-temperature (300 K).

Neutron, prompt gamma ray, and delayed gamma ray energy spectra and fluence per fission were
calculated using a surface tally of a volumetric space sphere for the central cavity or a 6-cm diameter
surface tally sphere for the ex-core locations. In Figure 6, all the characterized positions are noted with
a white sphere. Calculations were performed using the MCNP k-code mode for the neutron and
prompt gamma ray environments and in the source mode for the delayed gamma ray environment.

 21

Figure 8. MCNP model of Godiva IV showing elevation and plane views, respectively.

 22

3. SPECTRUM CHARACTERZATION

3.1. Neutron
The neutron environment includes both prompt and delayed neutrons from the Godiva IV core. The
neutron energy spectrum is first calculated using MCNP, and then a least-squares spectrum adjustment
is performed using passive neutron activation dosimetry measurements to produce a “characterized”
neutron spectrum. The process for determining a characterized neutron spectrum is to first generate
an a priori 640-group and an 89-group neutron energy trial spectrum from the MCNP model presented
in Section 2 for the 6-cm diameter tally sphere. To provide reasonable statistics in all of the energy
groups, the calculation was performed on a parallel machine for 20 billion source neutrons. The 89-
group MCNP results represents the trial function that is then adjusted using a least-squares-based
unfolding code. The 89-group energy structure is the energy grouping that can also be used in the
NuGET code. The 640-group structure if the fine structure spectra that can be used to re-bin in this
analysis. For this work a total of 195 foils were irradiated at Godiva IV resulting in 46 different
measured transmutation reactions. The LSL-M2 code and the IRDFF-II dosimetry cross sections,
were used in the unfolding analysis to determine the characterized neutron energy spectrum [2][14].

3.1.1. MCNP Model Neutron Energy Spectra Results
The neutron energy spectrum was calculated for a 640-group and an 89-group energy structure using
the MCNP model presented in Section 2 for a 6-cm diameter tally sphere at each of the four locations.
As noted previously, MCNP 6.3 was used with the ENDF/B-VIII.0 cross sections. The MCNP model
used for all of these environments can be found in Appendix A of this report. The calculations were
performed in the MCNP k-code mode using both neutrons and photons in the particle and tally
tracking flags. This arrangement allowed for both the neutron energy spectrum and the prompt
gamma ray energy spectrum to be generated in one simulation. The gamma ray spectrum uses a 48-
group energy structure for binning. The results generated in the tally sphere are in units of neutron
fluence per source neutron. These results are then converted to fluence per fission then fluence per
MJ of reactor power using a value of 192.4 MeV per fission. This value represents the fission fragment,
neutron, prompt gamma ray, capture gamma ray and delayed gamma ray energy deposition in the
reactor core, per fission event. These energy deposition values were calculated using MCNP except
for the delayed gamma ray energy, which was assumed to be the total delayed gamma energy of 6.33
MeV per fission as characterized in the ENDF/B-VIII.0 nuclear data file. The 640-group neutron
energy spectrum result was used as the trial function for the LSL-M2 code.

Figure 9 through Figure 16 shows the MCNP-generated 640-group and 89-group neutron energy
fluences on both linear and logarithmic y-axis scales, for the 4 environments characterized at Godiva
IV. The units on all the y-axes are in lethargy fluence or energy fluence, equal to E dϕ/dE (MeV/MeV-
cm2-MJ). With the energy fluence represented linearly on the y-axis and the neutron energy on the x-
axis represented logarithmically, the area under the curve represents the total neutron fluence. This
representation allows for the best visual depiction of the fluence over the complete neutron energy
range.

 23

Figure 9. MCNP 640-Group and 89-Group Neutron Lethargy Fluence Energy Spectra for the In-
 Core location (Linear-Logarithmic).

Figure 10. MCNP 640-Group and 89-Group Neutron Lethargy Fluence Energy Spectra for the In-
 Core location (Logarithmic-Logarithmic).

 24

Figure 11. MCNP 640-Group and 89-Group Neutron Lethargy Fluence Energy Spectra for the Top
 Hat location (Linear-Logarithmic).

Figure 12. MCNP 640-Group and 89-Group Neutron Lethargy Fluence Energy Spectra for the Top
 Hat location (Logarithmic-Logarithmic).

 25

Figure 13. MCNP 640-Group and 89-Group Neutron Lethargy Fluence Energy Spectra for the 1m
 location (Linear-Logarithmic).

Figure 14. MCNP 640-Group and 89-Group Neutron Lethargy Fluence Energy Spectra for the 1m
 location (Logarithmic-Logarithmic).

 26

Figure 15. MCNP 640-Group and 89-Group Neutron Lethargy Fluence Energy Spectra for the 2m
 location (Linear-Logarithmic).

Figure 16. MCNP 640-Group and 89-Group Neutron Lethargy Fluence Energy Spectra for the 2m
 location (Logarithmic-Logarithmic).

 27

The energy fluence for the In-Core environment in Godiva-IV does not have an easily identifiable
thermal peak. For the Top Hat, 1m, and 2m, the thermal peak occurs at 5E-08 MeV. The fission peak
for all four locations appears at the same energy, roughly 1.1 MeV. The results for the 640-group and
89-group neutron energy fluence are in very close agreement, as expected. The 640-group neutron
energy calculation in some cases shows more structure in the fluence spectrum, which is more notably
observed in the energy range of 1E-2 MeV to 1 MeV. This structure is considered to be real and not
an artifact of the code or cross-section set. It is caused by resonances in the cross sections, especially
from the oxygen elastic scattering cross section and how the cross-sections are binned. The structure
in the energy range below 0.01 eV (1.0E-8 MeV) indicates uncertainty in the MCNP results in this
energy range are greater than 10%. This structure indicates that the Monte Carlo statistics are poor
and that the results have a low confidence. The calculated standard deviation from MCNP for each
energy bin in the 640-group energy fluence in the energy range from 0.005 eV (5.0E-9 MeV) to 6 MeV
is less than 0.1%. A similar but less resolved structure is also seen in the 89-group energy fluence.

3.1.2. Dosimetry Foils
The results of the MCNP calculation of the Godiva IV in-core and ex-core free-field environment
represent a good initial representation for the neutron spectrum characterization. However, a
considerable uncertainty in the results can exist due to model representation uncertainty (geometry,
density, and composition) and uncertainty in the transport cross sections. An improved spectrum can
be obtained by combining this initial trial spectrum with measured integral values that correspond to
the reaction rates from high-fidelity passive dosimetry reactions. The resulting least-squares adjusted
neutron fluence spectrum includes an energy-dependent uncertainty. The spectrum can then be
considered “characterized,” in that it quantifies the “true” neutron-fluence energy spectrum with a
stated accuracy, including energy dependent uncertainties and a covariance matrix. If the least-squares
spectrum is found to have inconsistent inputs in comparing the MCNP a priori trial spectrum and the
measured dosimetry results, as determined by a measure of the chi-squared per degrees of freedom
(c2/dof) in the spectrum adjustment, the dosimetry measurements would be reexamined and/or the
MCNP model for Godiva IV and environments would be reexamined and modified or modeled with
greater fidelity. In addition to the adjusted neutron spectrum, uncertainty and covariance results are
found from the least-squares analysis using LSL-M2. The characterized neutron spectrum can be used,
with the corresponding uncertainty values and the correlation matrix, to support the evaluation of
various damage and dose metrics relevant to users of Godiva IV free-field environment. The
characterized spectrum can be used directly by the NuGET code or as an isotropic shell source in
MCNP to calculate the radiation internal to a complex test object. For this work, the LSL-M2 code
was used to generate a characterized 89-group neutron energy spectrum.

The selection of passive dosimetry foils and activation reactions has been studied and evaluated over
many years. A summary of the work can be found in ASTM E720 and references therein [11]. No
complete and perfect set of activation reactions exists that allows the neutron fluence energy spectrum
to be calculated by dosimetry alone. However, there are enough reactions to cover the relevant energy
range with high-fidelity dosimetry cross sections to allow for adjusted neutron fluence results to be
generated with a quantified accuracy. The passive dosimetry foils and the associated neutron activities,
at the end of irradiation, used to perform the neutron fluence characterization for the in-core and ex-
core free-field environments are shown in Table 2, Table 3, Table 4, and Table 5.

The foils and activation reactions chosen for the measurement and analysis represent expert judgment
and references to previous work, including characterization of the ACRR Free-Field [13], and
historical characterization of the ACRR free-field condition and the LB36 bucket [10]. A complete set
of dosimetry foils and reaction data will vary for the given environment being characterized. Typically,
neutron activation resulting in the emission of protons (n,p), neutrons (n,2n), (n,n’), or alpha particles
(n,a) represent high neutron-energy reactions of 1 MeV or greater. Neutron activation resulting in
prompt gamma-ray emission from radiative capture (n,g) or fission reactions determine the shape of
the thermal and epithermal region of the neutron spectrum. Covering foils with cadmium (Cd) can
allow for resonances above the associated cutoff energies to become more prominent, allowing for
additional information to be included in the analysis.

A total of 195 different foil types, resulting in 46 different reactions, were irradiated at the Godiva IV.
The foils were irradiated in multiple burst and steady-state operations that resulted in equivalent power
yields (~1.7 MJ over the operation). The five fission foils (U-235, three U-238, and Np-237) were
irradiated together in a free-field configuration in one steady-state operation. One foil, copper (Cu)
was also irradiated but several reactions were not used in the analysis due to significant inconsistencies
in the unfolding results. It is unclear if there is a problem in the measurement technique for these
reactions, foil pre-exposure issues, or if there are issues with the dosimetry cross sections. Also, one
aluminum reaction measured for the In-Core analysis, was deemed suspect as the counting occurred
after 45 minutes, and this aluminum reaction has a half-life of 9.458 minutes.

Figure 17 shows a picture of some of the dosimetry foils used. The irradiations were all performed at
the peak axial neutron fluence location within the central cavity. From sulfur tablet irradiations,
presented in Section 2.1.1 of this report, the peak axial fast neutron fluence is in a square position
roughly 3.25 inches from Godiva IV stand top plate, on the top hat. Multiple foils were irradiated on
prop up stands around the core, with foils held above the floor at 72 inches (6 ft) on a 1.5-inch by 2-
inch test space on each stand. To minimize any self-shielding effects, the foils were never stacked
together. Instead, the foils were arranged on the surface of an aluminum plate held in place with
double-sided Kapton tape. For each irradiation, at least one nickel foil was irradiated and used to
normalize all of the irradiations. Several types of foils were used for each irradiation, and multiple foils
of the same type were tested during the campaign. An average of the nickel foil activity was used to
generate the normalization constant required for other foils.

 33

 Figure 17. Pictures of the foil layout. Top-Left: The 2m layout for the bare foil irradiations. Top-
Right: orientation of the fission foils on the Top Hat. Bottom-Center: In-Core foil order with nickel
 foil #1 being closer to the center of Godiva IV.

3.1.3. LSL Spectrum Unfold Results
The LSL-M2 code was run using the a priori 640-energy group trial spectra from the In-Core, Top
Hat, 1m and 2m locations from MCNP and the dosimetry data presented in Table 2. Also required in
the analysis was an initial uncertainty estimate in the neutron spectrum as a function of energy, an
initial energy-dependent correlation matrix, the energy dependent self-shielding factors, and the
dosimetry cross section library that also included uncertainties and covariance matrices. The input
deck used for the LSL calculation is included in Appendix B of this report. In addition to the counting
uncertainty, an additional 4% uncertainty was included for the foils to address uncertainty
contributions due to positioning and possible geometrical effects in the test setups. The output from
LSL is the 89-energy group NuGET format described earlier.

The LSL adjusted results for the number fraction, percent standard deviation, and covariance as a
function of the 89-group energy structure for the Godiva IV characterized locations are included in
an LSL format in Appendix C of this report. It should be noted that portions of the energy spectra
for all of the environment locations are highly correlated, so the uncertainty in the integral metric can
be much less than the average uncertainty. If required, users should perform uncertainty propagation
using the full energy-dependent covariance matrix given in Appendix C to determine the correlated
uncertainty.

3.1.4. In-Core Neutron Energy Spectrum Results
For the Godiva IV In-Core irradiation location, the resulting value for c2/dof is 1.027, which
represents an acceptable value. Figure 18 and Figure 19 show the same representation of the neutron
energy spectra as previously shown in Figure 9 and Figure 10, with the additional 89-energy group
adjusted spectrum result from the LSL analysis (light blue curve). Figure 20 shows the percent
adjustment made to the trial spectrum (black line), and the percent standard deviation in the adjusted
spectrum (purple line). For the In-Core location, it should be noted that due to the fast neutron
environment of Godiva IV, the percent adjustment in the thermal neutron region is not well known
or resolved. The adjusted neutron fluence in Figure 18 and Figure 19 shows a higher value in the
thermal energy range (1E-8 to 3E-6 MeV) than calculated using the MCNP model. This difference

 34

could be due to a number of factors including accuracy of the geometry and density of materials in
the Godiva IV MCNP model, cross section or scattering model discrepancies in MCNP.

Figure 21 shows the dosimetry foil relative reaction probabilities for the In-Core as a function of
energy from the LSL analysis. Each relative reaction probability sums to 100%. It is desirable to have
diverse coverage over the complete energy range. However, due to the fast neutron nature of the In-
core environment, both the computational sampling and dosimetry activation in the thermal neutron
region was minimal or undetectable respectively. Peaks represent areas of higher probability that allow
for energy discrimination in the analysis. Figure 21 shows that there is good coverage in the fast region
with many peaks in the energy range between 200 keV and 1.5 MeV, and 1 MeV to 10 MeV. There is
less coverage in the 1 keV to 1 MeV energy range.

Table 6 shows the result of the calculated to experimental (C/E) dosimetry results for the trial
spectrum and the adjusted spectrum. Also shown is the factor adjusted from the trial spectrum to the
adjusted spectrum for the dosimetry reactions. For the high-energy reactions, the C/E-1 values are
found to be both positive and negative for the trial spectrum and are reduced for the adjusted
spectrum. For the radiative capture reactions, the C/E-1 values are all positive, apart from a single
reaction, for the trial spectrum. This infers that the thermal/epithermal calculated fluence is too high.
After the adjustment, the results become much closer to zero for C/E-1.

The results for several integral metrics and conversion factors are shown in Table 8. The total neutron
fluence is normalized to 1.00 and the other values for fluence are in reference to this value. These
values were calculated as part of the LSL analysis. Conversion values to translate to n/cm2 are given
for fissions in the reactor, MJ of reactor energy, and 58Ni(n,p)58Co activity at the characterized location
in the cavity. To maintain consistency, the experimenter should always use a reference fast neutron
reaction or reactions, e.g. 58Ni(n,p)58Co and 32S(n,p) 32P, as a normalizing metric between operations.
Typically, Ni, S, and TLD sensors are included in an experiment package in order to normalize the
results of the experiment. Figure 20 showed the energy-dependent spectrum uncertainty. It must be
noted that portions of the energy spectrum are highly correlated, so the uncertainty in the integral
metric can be much less than the average uncertainty.

3.1.5. Top Hat Neutron Energy Spectrum Results
For the Godiva IV Top Hat irradiation location, the resulting value for c2/dof is 1.882, which
represents an acceptable value. Figure 22 and Figure 23 show the same representation of the neutron
energy spectra as previously shown in Figure 11 and Figure 12, with the additional 89-energy group
adjusted spectrum result from the LSL analysis (light blue curve). Figure 24 shows the percent
adjustment made to the trial spectrum (black line), and the percent standard deviation in the adjusted
spectrum (purple line). The adjusted neutron fluence in Figure 20 shows a lower value in the thermal
energy range (1E-8 to 3E-6 MeV) than calculated using the MCNP model. This difference could be
due to a number of factors including accuracy of the geometry and density of materials in the Godiva
IV MCNP model, cross section or scattering model discrepancies in MCNP.

Figure 25 shows the dosimetry foil relative reaction probabilities as a function of energy from the LSL
analysis. Each relative reaction probability sums to 100%. It is desirable to have diverse coverage over
the complete energy range. Peaks represent areas of higher probability that allow for energy
discrimination in the analysis. The cadmium cutoff value is at 0.5 eV. Figure 25 shows that there is
good coverage with many peaks in the energy range between 0.001 eV and 1 keV, and 1 MeV to 10
MeV. There is less coverage in the range 1 keV to 1 MeV.

 42

Table 10 shows the result of the calculated to experimental (C/E) dosimetry results for the trial
spectrum and the adjusted spectrum. Also shown is the factor adjusted from the trial spectrum to the
adjusted spectrum for the dosimetry reactions. For the high-energy reactions, the C/E-1 values are
found to be both positive and negative for the trial spectrum and are reduced for the adjusted
spectrum. For the radiative capture reactions, the C/E-1 values are all positive, with the exception of
a single reaction, for the trial spectrum. This infers that the thermal/epithermal calculated fluence is
too high. After the adjustment, the results become much closer to zero for C/E-1.

standard deviation for the adjusted spectrum. The number fraction, represented as a histogram, can

energy is calculated to be 1.355 MeV. The peak differential number fraction occurs in energy group 5
at 0.03 eV. The differential fluence at 1 MeV (Group 74) is almost three orders of magnitude lower
than the peak. The differential energy fluence for the same comparison is about the same order of
magnitude as for the 1 MeV value.

The results for a number of integral metrics and conversion factors are shown in Table 12. The total
neutron fluence is normalized to 1.00 and the other values for fluence are in reference to this value.
These values were calculated as part of the LSL analysis. Conversion values to translate to n/cm2 are
given for fissions in the reactor, MJ of reactor energy, and 58Ni(n,p)58Co activity at the characterized
location in the cavity. In order to maintain consistency, the experimenter should always use a reference
fast neutron reaction or reactions, e.g. 58Ni(n,p)58Co and 32S(n,p) 32P, as a normalizing metric between
operations. Typically, Ni, S, and TLD sensors are included in an experiment package in order to
normalize the results of the experiment. Figure 18 showed the energy-dependent spectrum
uncertainty. It must be noted that portions of the energy spectrum are highly correlated, so the
uncertainty in the integral metric can be much less than the average uncertainty.

3.1.6. 1m Neutron Energy Spectrum Results
For the Godiva IV 1m irradiation location, the resulting value for c2/dof is 1.200, which represents
an acceptable value. Figure 26 and Figure 27 show the same representation of the neutron energy
spectra as previously shown in Figure 13 and Figure 14 with the additional 89-energy group adjusted
spectrum result from the LSL analysis (light blue curve). Figure 20 shows the percent adjustment made
to the trial spectrum (black line), and the percent standard deviation in the adjusted spectrum (purple
line). The adjusted neutron fluence in Figure 20 shows a lower value in the thermal energy range (1E-
8 to 3E-6 MeV) than calculated using the MCNP model. This difference could be due to a number of
factors including accuracy of the geometry and density of materials in the Godiva IV MCNP model,
cross section or scattering model discrepancies in MCNP.

Figure 29 shows the dosimetry foil relative reaction probabilities as a function of energy from the LSL
analysis. Each relative reaction probability sums to 100%. It is desirable to have diverse coverage over
the complete energy range. Peaks represent areas of higher probability that allow for energy
discrimination in the analysis. Figure 29 shows all of the reactions used in the analysis. The cadmium
cutoff value is at 0.5 eV. Figure 21 shows that there is good coverage with many peaks in the energy
range between 0.001 eV and 1 keV, and 1 MeV to 10 MeV. There is less coverage in the range 1 keV
to 1 MeV.

 50

Table 14 shows the result of the calculated to experimental (C/E) dosimetry results for the trial
spectrum and the adjusted spectrum. Also shown is the factor adjusted from the trial spectrum to the
adjusted spectrum for the dosimetry reactions. For the high-energy reactions, the C/E-1 values are
found to be both positive and negative for the trial spectrum and are reduced for the adjusted
spectrum. For the radiative capture reactions, the C/E-1 values are all positive, with the exception of
a single reaction, for the trial spectrum. This infers that the thermal/epithermal calculated fluence is
too high. After the adjustment, the results become much closer to zero for C/E-1.

Table 15 shows the adjusted neutron fluence spectrum in a tabular format for the 89-energy group

standard deviation for the adjusted spectrum. The number fraction, represented as a histogram, can
be used in MCNP source calculations as an isotropic spherical surface source. The average neutron
energy is calculated to be 0.302 MeV. The peak differential number fraction occurs in energy group 5
at 0.03 eV. The differential fluence at 1 MeV (Group 74) is almost eight orders of magnitude lower
than the peak. The differential energy fluence for the same comparison is about the same order of
magnitude as for the 1 MeV value.

The results for a number of integral metrics and conversion factors are shown in Table 16. The total
neutron fluence is normalized to 1.00 and the other values for fluence are in reference to this value.
These values were calculated as part of the LSL analysis. Conversion values to translate to n/cm2 are
given for fissions in the reactor, MJ of reactor energy, and 58Ni(n,p)58Co activity at the characterized
location in the cavity. In order to maintain consistency, the experimenter should always use a reference
fast neutron reaction or reactions, e.g. 58Ni(n,p)58Co and 32S(n,p) 32P, as a normalizing metric between
operations. Typically, Ni, S, and TLD sensors are included in an experiment package in order to
normalize the results of the experiment. Figure 18 showed the energy-dependent spectrum
uncertainty. It must be noted that portions of the energy spectrum are highly correlated, so the
uncertainty in the integral metric can be much less than the average uncertainty.

3.1.7. 2m Neutron Energy Spectrum Results
For the Godiva IV 2m irradiation location, the resulting value for c2/dof is 1.020, which represents
an acceptable value. Figure 30 and Figure 31 show the same representation of the neutron energy
spectra as previously shown in Figure 15 and Figure 16 with the additional 89-energy group adjusted
spectrum result from the LSL analysis (light blue curve). Figure 20 shows the percent adjustment made
to the trial spectrum (black line), and the percent standard deviation in the adjusted spectrum (purple
line). The adjusted neutron fluence in Figure 20 shows a lower value in the thermal energy range (1E-
8 to 3E-6 MeV) than calculated using the MCNP model. This difference could be due to a number of
factors including accuracy of the geometry and density of materials in the Godiva IV MCNP model,
cross section or scattering model discrepancies in MCNP.

Figure 33 shows the dosimetry foil relative reaction probabilities as a function of energy from the LSL
analysis. Each relative reaction probability sums to 100%. It is desirable to have diverse coverage over
the complete energy range. Peaks represent areas of higher probability that allow for energy
discrimination in the analysis. The cadmium cutoff value is at 0.5 eV. Figure 33 shows that there is
good coverage with many peaks in the energy range between 0.001 eV and 1 keV, and 1 MeV to 10
MeV. There is less coverage in the range 1 keV to 1 MeV.

 58

Table 18 shows the result of the calculated to experimental (C/E) dosimetry results for the trial
spectrum and the adjusted spectrum. Also shown is the factor adjusted from the trial spectrum to the
adjusted spectrum for the dosimetry reactions. For the high-energy reactions, the C/E-1 values are
found to be both positive and negative for the trial spectrum and are reduced for the adjusted
spectrum. For the radiative capture reactions, the C/E-1 values are all positive, with the exception of
a single reaction, for the trial spectrum. This infers that the thermal/epithermal calculated fluence is
too high. After the adjustment, the results become much closer to zero for C/E-1.

Table 19 shows the adjusted neutron fluence spectrum in a tabular format for the 89-energy group

standard deviation for the adjusted spectrum. The number fraction, represented as a histogram, can
be used in MCNP source calculations as an isotropic spherical surface source. The average neutron
energy is calculated to be 0.302 MeV. The peak differential number fraction occurs in energy group 5
at 0.03 eV. The differential fluence at 1 MeV (Group 74) is almost eight orders of magnitude lower
than the peak. The differential energy fluence for the same comparison is about the same order of
magnitude as for the 1 MeV value. The LSL adjusted results for the number fraction, percent standard
deviation, and covariance as a function of the 89-group energy structure are included in an LSL format
in Appendix C of this report.

The results for a number of integral metrics and conversion factors are shown in Table 20. The total
neutron fluence is normalized to 1.00 and the other values for fluence are in reference to this value.
These values were calculated as part of the LSL analysis. Conversion values to translate to n/cm2 are
given for fissions in the reactor, MJ of reactor energy, and 58Ni(n,p)58Co activity at the characterized
location in the cavity. In order to maintain consistency, the experimenter should always use a reference
fast neutron reaction or reactions, e.g. 58Ni(n,p)58Co and 32S(n,p) 32P, as a normalizing metric between
operations. Typically, Ni, S, and TLD sensors are included in an experiment package in order to
normalize the results of the experiment. Figure 18 showed the energy-dependent spectrum
uncertainty. It must be noted that portions of the energy spectrum are highly correlated, so the
uncertainty in the integral metric can be much less than the average uncertainty. Users should perform
uncertainty propagation using the full energy-dependent covariance matrix given in Appendix C in
order to determine the correlated uncertainty.

3.2. Prompt-Gamma Ray
The gamma-ray environment includes both prompt and delayed gamma rays. Prompt gamma-rays are
generated from the direct fission process in the Godiva IV fuel, and from radiative capture and
inelastic scattering of neutrons in the Godiva IV fuel and surrounding structural materials. Delayed
gamma-rays are generated by fission-product decay in the Godiva IV fuel and decay from activated
materials in the core and structural support material. All gamma-rays are considered to fall into one
of these two categories, prompt or delayed. Gamma-rays generated from the direct fission process as
the reactor power decreases following a pulse are considered prompt gamma-rays.

It is not currently practical to use experimental integral measurements to adjust the gamma-ray energy
spectrum as it is for neutrons, using the current state-of-the-art technology in gamma-ray detection
and energy-deposition measurements. For photon energies above the K-edges for photon cross
sections (~80 keV), most material response functions fail to show any strong energy-dependent
structure that can be used to resolve the spectrum. There is simply no effective way to discriminate
energy groups in a gamma-ray fluence within a small volume in a reactor core. Integral dose
measurements can be performed and are used as benchmarks for calculated integral responses. These
integral measurements have value in validation but are not useful for spectrum adjustment. However,

 66

both passive dosimeters (e.g., TLDs) and active dosimeters (e.g., calorimeters) have some neutron and
delayed gamma-ray contribution effects that make deciphering the results much more complex.
The prompt gamma-ray fluences can be calculated in a similar way as was performed for the neutron
environment. With both neutrons and photons (gamma-rays) turned “on” in the k-code mode of
MCNP, the prompt gamma-rays generated by fission, neutron radiative capture, and neutron inelastic
scattering will be tracked properly, without any additional input from the user. The production and
energy spectrum for the source fission gamma-rays are modeled for the MCNP 6.3/ENDF-supplied
.00c fissile and fissionable cross sections. The radiative capture cross sections for neutron absorption
are also modeled, but special attention should be made to ensure the gamma-ray production cross
section is included in the cross-section data for each reaction of interest. This information for each
individual isotope can be found in Appendix G of the MCNP Data Libraries of the MCNP manual.
Typically, it is estimated that there are about seven gamma-rays generated in the fission process at
energies of about 1 MeV each. Another 1 to 12 MeV of radiative capture gamma-rays can be emitted.
This value is dependent on the type of reactor and absorbing materials in the fuel elements, structure,
and control elements. For Godiva IV, the total energy calculated for the prompt gamma-rays (fission,
radiative capture, and inelastic scattering) is ~13 MeV per fission.

3.2.1. In-Core Prompt Gamma-Ray Energy Spectrum Results
Figure 34 and Figure 35 show the MCNP generated 48-energy group prompt gamma-ray fluence for
the Godiva IV In-Core location on a linear and logarithmic y-axis, respectively. The units on the y-
axis are in energy fluence, equal to E dϕ/dE (MeV/MeV-cm2-MJ). With the energy fluence
represented linearly on the y-axis and the photon energy on the x-axis represented logarithmically, the
area under the curve represents the total prompt gamma-ray fluence. The gamma-ray energy fluence
has a prominent peak at ~0.5 MeV and another peak at ~0.8 MeV.

3.2.2. Top Hat Prompt Gamma-Ray Energy Spectrum Results
Figure 34 and Figure 35 show the MCNP generated 48-energy group prompt gamma-ray fluence for
the Godiva IV Top Hat location on a linear and logarithmic y-axis, respectively. The units on the y-
axis are in energy fluence, equal to E dϕ/dE (MeV/MeV-cm2-MJ). With the energy fluence
represented linearly on the y-axis and the photon energy on the x-axis represented logarithmically, the
area under the curve represents the total prompt gamma-ray fluence. The gamma-ray energy fluence
has a prominent peak at ~0.5 MeV and another peak at ~0.8 MeV.

3.3. Delayed-Gamma Ray Results
Delayed gamma-rays are generated by fission-product decay in Godiva IV fuel and by the decay of
activated materials in the reactor core. MCNP does not intrinsically calculate the source for these
gamma-rays. MCNP6.3 can be used with the burnup feature of the code to generate fission products
and activation products in defined regions of the core. But in order to generate the decay gamma-ray
source, a separate calculation would need to be performed that would generate the time-dependent or
time-integrated gamma-ray energy spectrum from the unique isotopic inventory and gamma-ray lines.
For the analysis presented in this report, a much simpler approach was taken. The assumption was
made that only early-time fission product generated decay gamma-rays would be modeled. This model
ignores the gamma-ray contribution from neutron activation in the core. A generic fission product
decay gamma-ray energy spectrum was used in the MCNP analysis and transported the gamma-rays
from the fission distribution in the Godiva IV fuel to the detector sphere in the central cavity.

The total delayed gamma-ray energy released from the fission products used was 6.33 MeV per fission
for U-235 fission from ENDF/B-VIII.0. This value is about half of the value emitted as prompt
gamma-rays and therefore cannot be ignored as a trivial quantity. The number of delayed gamma-rays

 85

emitted per fission was then calculated to be 6.57 delayed photons/fission, using the source delayed
gamma-ray energy spectrum for fast U-235 fission.

Figure 42 shows the delayed gamma-ray energy source distribution in units of gamma-rays per MeV
as a function of energy. This spectrum was used in a histogram format for the source calculation in
MCNP described above. The resulting transported gamma-ray fluence to the tally sphere is in units of
fluence per source delayed gamma-ray. Although fission products continue to decay over many years,
the majority of the energy (~85%) is released from the shorter-lived products over 1E4 seconds (2.8
hours). The fractional energy release up to 0.1 s is ~1%, ~6.5% up to 1 s, ~23.2% up to 10 s, and
~55% up to 300 s (5 minutes).

 Figure 42. Delayed Gamma-Ray Source Distribution Used for MCNP Analysis.

The decay gamma-ray fluences was calculated with MCNP using the source mode and with photons
only. The same model of the Godiva IV was used with a represented fission product gamma-ray
source energy spectrum. The same 48-energy group structure was used as for prompt gamma-rays.
The fission-product delayed gamma-ray energy spectrum used was for fast U-235 fission in the time
interval from 0.2 to 0.5 seconds [7] [8]. The gamma-ray source distribution within Godiva IV core
plates is distributed radially from fission sites. The distribution function was generated from a separate
MCNP k-code calculation by tallying the number of fissions in each row of fuel. A constant axial
source distribution was assumed. The results gave the transported delayed gamma-ray fluence
spectrum per source photon.

represented linearly on the y-axis and the photon energy on the x-axis represented logarithmically, the

3.3.2. Top Hat Delayed Gamma-Ray Energy Spectrum Results
For the Godiva IV Top Hat location Figure 43 and Figure 44 show the MCNP generated 48-energy
group delayed gamma-ray fluence on a linear and logarithmic y-axis, respectively. The units on the y-
axis are in energy fluence, equal to E dϕ/dE (MeV/MeV-cm2-MJ). With the energy fluence

area under the curve represents the total gamma-ray fluence. The scale of the plots is the same as
presented for the prompt gamma-rays. The delayed gamma-ray energy fluence has a prominent peak
at ~0.5 MeV.

For the Godiva IV 2m location Figure 49 and Figure 50 show the MCNP generated 48-energy group
delayed gamma-ray fluence on a linear and logarithmic y-axis, respectively. The units on the y-axis are
in energy fluence, equal to E dϕ/dE (MeV/MeV-cm2-MJ). With the energy fluence represented
linearly on the y-axis and the photon energy on the x-axis represented logarithmically, the area under
the curve represents the total gamma-ray fluence. The scale of the plots is the same as presented for
the prompt gamma-rays. The delayed gamma-ray energy fluence has a prominent peak at ~0.5 MeV.

 97

 Figure 49. MCNP 48-Group Delayed Gamma-Ray Energy Spectrum (Linear–Logarithmic).

  Figure 50. MCNP 48-Group Delayed Gamma-Ray Energy Spectrum (Logarithmic-Logarithmic).

Table 36 shows the delayed gamma-ray fluence spectrum in a tabular format for the 48-energy group

calculations as an isotropic spherical surface source. The average delayed gamma-ray energy is
calculated to be 1.008 MeV. The peak differential energy fluence again occurs in energy group 14 at
0.5125 MeV. This is the large peak seen in Figure 49 and represents the electron-positron annihilation
photon energy (0.511 MeV).

4. OPERATIONS
The photon and neutron field characterization were performed at three locations external to the
Godiva IV core (one meter southeast from the center of Godiva IV, two meters northeast from the
center of Godiva IV, and on the outer surface of the Top Hat) and one location internal to the
Godiva IV core (central cavity). The characterization experiments consisted of a total of three
steady-state operations and four 250 °C burst operations. Each steady-state operation ran for
approximately 30 minutes at a nominal 600 W to produce an equivalent number of fissions as a 250
°C burst.

The data for this report was assembled over the span of 4 weeks, with preliminary measurements
conducted in conjunction with a non-NCSP criticality safety class, one week of equipment setup and
testing, and two weeks of dedicated measurements.

103

5. CONCLUSIONS

This report presents the characterized neutron, prompt gamma ray, and delayed gamma ray radiation
environments for the free-field environment surrounding the Godiva IV critical assembly. The
characterized locations include the In-Core (central cavity), Top Hat, 1m, and 2m testing locations
commonly used withing the reactor cell. A 640-group and 89-group neutron energy spectrum, a 48-
group prompt gamma ray energy spectrum, and a 48-group delayed gamma ray energy spectrum were
calculated using high-fidelity MCNP model of Godiva IV. The neutron spectrum was adjusted to align
more closely with neutron activation dosimetry collected between November 29, 2022 to January 27,
2023. The adjustment was performed using an SNL modified version of the least-squares code LSL-
M2. Neutron, prompt gamma ray, and delayed gamma ray conversion factors are presented to facilitate
the conversion of various dosimetry readings into radiation metrics desired by the experimenter.
